pressure on borrowing costs just as many companies
face refinancing needs. The impact on profitability
is material. We estimate that a 50bps increase in the
average interest rate on corporate debt would reduce
the net margin of non-financial corporations from 9.0%
to 8.6% in the US and from 6.6% to 6.2% in Europe,
ceteris paribus. With a 100bps increase, margins fall
further to 8.4% and 6.0%, respectively – a compression

of roughly 0.6pp or 60bps in both regions. The burden
is not evenly distributed. Exposure to higher rates is
concentrated in capital-intensive, real-asset sectors
with large debt stocks and persistent refinancing
requirements. Under a 100bps shock, the net margin of
US utilities falls from 19% to about 17%, while interest
coverage declines from 3.1x to 2.4x. For European
utilities, margins fall more sharply, from 10% to 7%, and
coverage drops from 2.0x to 1.5x. US telecoms also see

interest coverage fall substantially, from 4.5x to 2.8x,
compared with a decline from 2.0x to 1.7x in Europe
(see Figure 4). The implication is that the next phase
of pressure on corporate margins may come less from
the initial energy shock itself than from its second-
round effects on financing conditions. Companies that
entered this period with high capital intensity, limited
pricing power and large refinancing needs are therefore
increasingly exposed to a combination of weaker

operating leverage and higher debt-service costs.
The corporate response is increasingly one of
balance-sheet and industrial-footprint adjustment
rather than conventional cyclical hedging.  That is
consistent with companies treating both the higher-
rate environment and the emerging tariff architecture
as durable features of the operating landscape rather
than temporary shocks. Automakers are shifting the
focus of margin defense away from unit growth and

towards cost reductions, platform simplification and
lifecycle revenues. Chemicals groups are accelerating
capacity rationalization and redirecting investment
towards structurally advantaged geographies rather
than waiting for the cycle to turn. Metals producers,
meanwhile, are maintaining capital discipline even
as electrification-related demand increasingly strains
available supply. These strategies have a common
objective: preserving cash generation and balance-

sheet capacity through a period in which neither
demand nor financing conditions can be assumed to
normalize quickly. The emphasis is therefore moving
from maximizing volume growth to protecting returns
on capital, liquidity and strategic flexibility. This also
exposes a growing asymmetry in how markets are
pricing corporate risk. The upside associated with
the AI-linked cluster is being capitalized immediately
through stronger earnings expectations and investment

flows. By contrast, the downside from higher energy
costs, tariffs and interest rates is concentrated in the
other two clusters – and particularly in Europe, where
structural energy disadvantages, weaker pricing power
in parts of industry and greater sensitivity to external
trade shocks compound the effect. The result is not
simply a divergence between sectors, but a widening
gap between companies able to fund structural growth
and those forced to manage structural adjustment. That

distinction is likely to matter increasingly for margins,
investment and corporate balance sheets going
forward.

### Page 21

28 September 2026
Global business insolvencies are running +6% above
year-ago levels year-to-date, extending what is now
the sixteenth consecutive quarter of y/y increases
and leaving levels across most markets well above
pre-pandemic norms. Among the largest economies,
the US leads with a +19% surge, followed by Japan
(+7%), Germany (+7%) and France (+5%), while the UK
(-5%) and China (0%) are the notable exceptions. The
strain is especially visible at the top end of the corporate

spectrum: Insolvencies among firms with turnover
above EUR50mn hit 134 in Q2 2026, the third-highest
quarterly tally on record, bringing the count for the first
half of the year to 247 cases (+13% y/y), with Western
Europe accounting for nearly two-thirds of the total and
industrial sectors such as chemicals, automotive and
machinery equipment showing the sharpest deterioration.
Insolvencies are projected to rise by at least +4% globally
for the full year 2026 (the fifth consecutive year of

increases) before a tentative stabilization in 2027. North
America is set to be the largest regional contributor, with
US filings forecast at +7% but a candidate for upside
revision given strong first-half momentum; 27 countries
are already tracking above our full-year projections.
In Western Europe, the expected +2% increase in 2026
would be followed by a modest -3% decline in 2027, but
nine out of 17 markets are forecast within the -4%/+4%
range, pointing to quasi-stable insolvency levels that

remain historically elevated. Asia will see contrasting
trends, with most countries prolonging their rise, except
Australia, Singapore and Hong Kong. The full impact of
the Middle East shock has yet to feed through corporate
income statements: higher input and shipping costs,
weak(er) consumer sentiment, elevated selling-price
expectations and higher-for-longer interest rates all point
to persisting margin pressure in the coming quarters,
in particular for medium, small and very small firms.

Highly leveraged sectors are particularly vulnerable as
debt-servicing costs compound the profitability squeeze,
suggesting greater differentiation across countries and
industries depending on their exposure to war-related
spillovers and, conversely, their ability to benefit from
global tailwinds such as AI and defense spending.

Capital markets outlook:
Cruel (bond) summer coming to an end
Capital markets had a difficult summer as the energy
shock and renewed inflation concerns pushed
government bond yields sharply upwards to multi-year
highs. Equities proved more resilient: Strong earnings and
AI-related investment kept returns positive despite rising
rates, while credit spreads remained close to historical
tights, supported by solid corporate results. In currencies,

EURUSD stayed broadly range-bound, while emerging-
market assets benefited from improving fundamentals
but now offer less spread compensation. Private markets
are becoming more selective: Private equity remains
constrained by a record exit backlog, private debt
continues to deliver attractive income despite pockets of
stress, infrastructure is benefiting from the AI buildout and
real estate faces renewed refinancing pressure. Looking
ahead, we expect the summer repricing to prove orderly

rather than the start of a broader market break. As the
energy impulse fades in our baseline outlook, we see
scope for gradually declining long-term rates, while equity
returns should moderate as valuations normalize.
The summer selloff in long-term rates had three
synchronized drivers: the feedback loop between
energy prices and policy rate expectations, firmer
economic activity and a higher term premium. The
repricing of the rate path was the strongest driver. The

term premium was the swing factor capturing supply-
demand imbalances emerging over summer in a
government bonds market that is moved at the margin
by price-sensitive, leveraged investors that react to
tighter funding conditions. Firmer economic activity on
either side of the Atlantic acted as underlying upside
trend. But we think US late-cycle dynamics will resume
in 2027 as the energy impulse fades and growth returns
to potential. Markets will price rate cuts. Long-term

rates will anticipate lower growth, bringing 10y yields
to 4.5% in 2027 and 4.3% in 2028. The US term-premium
rebuild looks largely complete and is actively contained
by Treasury operations (buybacks and active Treasury
issuance). The hawkish Fed turn should itself re-anchor
the long end by restoring inflation-fighting credibility.

The Eurozone sits earlier in the cycle. But its growth
impulse is moderate and there is no overshoot above
potential to justify holding policy above neutral for long.

We see markets pricing a reversal in mid-2027, easing
German 10y yields to 3.2% in 2027 and 3.0% in 2028. Both
curves bear-steepened through the summer. We now see
potential for further steepening, especially in the Eurozone
where the market-priced policy path is most excessively
tight. In both markets, the belly is the sweet spot on a six
to twelve month horizon. It captures some of the policy-
driven decline, stays supported by economic activity and

carries less term-premium risk than the long end. At current
levels, intermediate maturities show the best combination
of positive real returns and convexity.
Within the Eurozone the risk premium now sits in
France rather than Italy or Spain. The OAT-Bund spread
reached highest value since the Euro crisis (around
100bps) as markets began pricing a scenario in which the
new president (election in April/May 2027) will struggle
to gain a majority in the parliament, creating further

fiscal uncertainty. The near-term risk stays skewed wider,
and 130bps is reachable if political fragility deepens to
levels not yet seen. BTP spreads stay contained, but the
tightening impulse is missing as NGEU support fades, and
upcoming elections are an upside risk we would treat as
temporary. There is no systemic fragmentation risk within
EGBs as yield movement remain synchronized. Political
fragility, not market fragmentation, will be the theme of

the coming months.
EURUSD traded within a relatively narrow range of
1.14 to 1.18 during the last quarter, largely continuing
the pattern observed in previous quarters. Traditional
drivers such as the expected interest rate differential
and valuation metrics suggest that this range-bound
behavior is likely to persist. Within this range, short-
term exchange rate movements were primarily driven
by developments in the US, whereas events such as the

recent German elections had only a limited impact on
the currency pair. We expect this pattern to continue.
With the US midterm elections likely to increase political
uncertainty, we believe EURUSD could move closer to the
upper end of its recent trading range by year-end.
Equity markets: the 2026 earnings boost gives way
to normalization. Equities have held up through the
summer oil shock, leaving year-to-date total returns at
+13% in the US and +10% for Europe. Earnings did the

work: 12-month forward EPS growth runs near +20%
in the US, +16% in the EMU and above +30% in EMs.
But valuation dispersion is the widest in a decade: The
US trades at 19.4x forward earnings against a 20-year
average of 16.6x; the Eurozone at 16.4x and EM at 9.9x.
Valuations argue for selectivity rather than retreat:
Earnings should keep delivering the equity risk premium,

but with PEs above average in the US and Japan, we
expect the valuation premium to deflate there as growth
slows and macro risks rise, while Europe and EMs –

where expectations are lowest and the downside most
discounted – offer similar earnings sensitivity at a lower
price. We stay constructive on equities and expect Q4
to consolidate rather than extend the rally, with returns
converging towards the long-term equity risk premium
as multiples normalize. At year end, full-year 2026 total
returns should reach +14% for the US and +15% for the
Europe, before a more normal year in 2027 with +8% and
+9%, respectively. Double-digit EPS growth carries the

forecast (US: +32%/+16%/+16% over FY1-FY3; Eurozone:
+22%/+12%/+12%). US earnings revisions (+7.1% since
July) outrun nominal GDP only thanks to technology,
~30% of the index; ex-tech, the S&P 500 tracks the
economy, so the market carries the downside if AI capex
or monetization disappoints – growth, not inflation, is
the risk. Eurozone consensus prices +14.7% EPS growth
off +4.6% nominal GDP , but macro upgrades, lower
valuations and already negative sentiment support it,

while Japan looks hyped after a +20%. With positioning
crowded and implied volatility below average,
convertibles and hedges are compelling to retain equity
and earnings upside while tailoring the downside to
tolerance.
Credit markets have remained broadly resilient
through 2026, with spreads holding near the tight
end of their long-term range. In investment grade (IG),
upgrades continue to outnumber downgrades, though
the net upgrade trend has slowed from its 2025 peak.

Solid growth is offsetting the higher interest burden,
holding credit metrics: Interest coverage went below
its long-term median but stabilized above 6x in the
US and 8x in Europe, while net leverage remains near
cycle highs. In high yield (HY), global default rates have
stabilized, albeit at a level still above their long-run
average. The market has differentiated, reflecting tight
spreads yet above-average default rates: BB and B
credits have outperformed, supported by high carry and

fading recession risk, while CCC spreads remain elevated
and are likely to stay so as the weakest issuers face the
highest refinancing costs in a higher-for-longer rate
environment.

In the US, earnings growth and supportive technicals
have underpinned the market, though heavy technology
issuance is drawing investor attention.  IG gross supply

reached nearly USD1.5trn year-to-date, up 30% on
2025, while net issuance almost doubled. Demand has
moderated from the high of the start of the year, but new
issuance book coverage above 3x suggests the market
can still absorb a sizeable calendar. With technology
set to account for around a quarter of new issuance
through 2026, its share of the IG index could rise towards
15% in 2030. Heavier supply against adequate demand
argues for drift rather than dislocation: We expect US IG

at 85bps by end-2026 and 90bps in 2027–28, with HY at
290bps, 315bps and 310bps. Euro credit is comparatively
insulated from this technology concentration and offers
a diversification option. Technology represents only 3% of
the Euro IG index, which should limit spillovers from tech-
specific volatility. Current tight spreads are driven by ‘yield’
demand, and are likely to continue in a higher-for-longer
rates environment. However, increasing rate volatility

started to weigh on spreads in the near term. We see EUR
IG at around 85bps in 2027–28 and HY at approximately
300bps and 295bps.
Emerging markets remain resilient to global shocks.
However, the path for EM sovereign spreads is
asymmetrically skewed towards widening, while energy
volatility and Fed hiking remain the drivers for EMs
locally. EM fundamentals continue to strengthen, with
sovereign rating trends clearly shifting toward upgrades,
supported by successful debt restructurings, IMF-backed

policy discipline and improving monetary policy credibility
across the asset class. The structural growth premium that
EMs hold over developed markets remains intact and is
expected to widen further, supporting debt sustainability
and increasing the resilience to higher energy prices and
US rates volatility. Robust fundamentals, combined with
sustained investor inflows, have pushed EM sovereign
spreads to multi-year tight levels, compressing the rating-

adjusted spread premium versus US corporates close to
negative territory. From current multi-year tight levels,
spreads are expected to widen modestly by year-end.
With spreads offering limited additional pick-up, EM
local-currency assets appear relatively more attractive
at current yield levels. Given their correlation with
US Treasury yields and the ongoing EM central bank
cycle, yields are expected to move broadly sideways,
reaching 630bps by year-end. While valuations look

more compelling in local currency markets, the recent
environment is less favorable as Fed policy expectations
and oil-price volatility remain the key drivers of local
currency performance.

### Page 27

28 September 2026
27
EM equities are the cheapest way into the AI value
chain. Consensus EPS growth of +72%, +23% and +10%
over FY1-FY3 is driven by semiconductors, and AI supports
around a quarter of Asian growth. Yet the market still
prices EM semis at a discount to US peers: Their combined
USD4trn market capitalization is below Nvidia alone,
and technology weighs 21% in MSCI ACWI, of which only
3.5% is for EM semis – despite their crucial role and early

monetization. SK Hynix and Samsung trade near 4x PE,
with new long-term contracts extending the outlook
and securing payback, whereas US semis command
three times the multiple for twice the growth. The cycle
has limits: We estimate AI capex to be roughly halfway
through and expect it to peak above USD1trn in 2028,
when normalizing returns on equity would imply a 20-
45% PE discount that our target already anticipates. We
forecast MSCI EM total returns of +31% in 2026, +12% in

2027 and +7% in 2028 – ahead of developed markets next
year, fading only as the semiconductor cycle matures.
Across the USD16.7trn private capital industry, two
forces are doing most of the sorting. Rates, having risen
sharply again on both sides of the Atlantic rather than
easing as underwritten, are pushing financing costs back
toward levels many portfolios were not built for, and AI is
rewriting which businesses deserve a premium and which
do not. Each corner of private capital wears this pressure

differently. Private equity is stuck at the exit, sitting on
a record backlog of unsold companies as chastened
investors keep fundraising in a multi-year drought. Private
debt still pays its keep, with direct lending returning
almost 11% last year, but cracks are showing among
smaller funds and the most AI-exposed borrowers, where
write-downs are creeping higher. Infrastructure is the
outlier success story, its 12-13% annual return leaning on
the USD5-7trn the world is expected to spend this decade

wiring up data centers for the AI boom. Real estate‘s
fragile recovery is now at risk of stalling as financing costs
move higher again instead of easing, and AI‘s dampening
effect on office demand leaves the sector doubly exposed.
The thread running through all four is dispersion. Capital
is flowing to contracted, well-covered, AI-aligned assets
and fleeing everything else, widening the gap between
winners and laggards to levels rarely seen.
Private equity‘s story in 2026 is increasingly one of

exits, or the lack of them. US buyout funds are still sitting
on some 13,300 unsold companies, most acquired in the
frothy days of 2020-21 at prices that assumed multiples
would keep rising. Instead, across US buyout deals,
multiple expansion contributed 37.3% of value creation in
the 2011-13 vintages but just 0.5% in 2020-22, with EBITDA
margin expansion and revenue growth now doing almost
all the work (Figure 9). That is a much harder way to earn

a return, and it shows up directly in the exit numbers. US
exits fell -46.3% in the second quarter, kept alive mainly
by a handful of large sales rather than a broad clearing
of the backlog, while dealmaking itself dropped -37.5%
as the Fed reopened the door to rate hikes and software,
previously the industry‘s favored sector, fell -65.7% on
AI-driven concerns about its growth outlook. With so
little cash flowing back to investors, distributions remain

thin and the incentive to hold rather than sell persists.
Europe tells a related story, deal value rose +6.9% in
the second quarter, but its own deals-to-exit ratio has
climbed from 2.4x to 2.9x, an early sign that the same
operational reckoning may be coming. The lesson on
both sides of the Atlantic is the same: Returns now have
to be built company by company rather than bought
through re-rating, and only sponsors who can genuinely
grow revenue and margins will convert their backlog into

distributions. We expect private equity to return 7-9% this
year, rising to 11-12% in 2027 and 2028 as the exit market
gradually reopens.

Private debt has spent 2026 absorbing shocks it was
largely built to withstand. Oil spikes and rate scares are

not new for this asset class. The summer‘s Hormuz-driven
energy shock pushed spreads a little wider, but the move
has been modest. Spreads remain close to historic lows,
and leveraged loan defaults, while ticking up, are still only
a small drag on returns. In this environment we expect
defaults to normalize gradually as energy prices and
rate expectations settle back down. Some of the recent
concern bundles together several separate issues – retail-

vehicle redemptions, loan quality, software exposure and
fund-wrapper design – and treating them as one story
overstates the risk. The more interesting divergence is in
valuations rather than credit quality. Private NAVs have
held steady, while listed vehicles trade near 17% discounts,
and payment-in-kind income, now around 8% of BDC
investment income, is edging up among the weakest
borrowers, whose interest coverage is squeezed by still-
high floating coupons. The sensible approach, as in most

elsewhere in the portfolio. We expect spreads to hold
around 550bps through year-end, cruising at similar levels
through 2027 and 2028 as the asset class settles into a
steadier, income-led phase.
Infrastructure is expected to sustain strong
performance, underpinned by AI buildout as a growth
lever. Data centers continue to dominate the deal market
this year. Despite strong new-build across major regions,
demand keeps outstripping supply (Figure 14), holding

vacancy at record lows while rents keep growing strongly.
With power, permitting, and land constraints sustaining
the scarcity, the economics stay attractive for ready-to-use
data centers contracted long-term to investment-grade
counterparties. Two tailwinds are strengthening the case.
First, the token economics are getting more compelling:
Usage keeps surging despite significant efficiency gains
as workloads shift from chatbots to agentic, reasoning-

heavy models and the paradigm grows more compute-
intensive. Token prices are stabilizing at the frontier
even as serving costs keep falling, supporting provider
margins. Hyperscaler AI revenue is now beginning to cover
the capex depreciation, pointing to a more sustainable
cycle. Second, rising data sovereignty demands across
regions add a further durable tailwind. We continue to
see infrastructure as a strong play in the AI supercycle –
the foundation on which the whole AI stack is built – with

tangible, contracted earnings rather than speculative
upside.

### Page 29

28 September 2026
29
The income-led recovery in real estate continues,
but higher long-end yields weigh on pricing and
refinancing. Liquidity is returning ahead of pricing:
Investment volumes are up 21% y/y in the US and 10%
in Europe, drifting back to pre-Covid norms, while prices
are stabilizing and cap rates are broadly flat. Values
remain 14-16% below the 2022 peak in aggregate on
both sides of the Atlantic, with US offices down more
than 40% and Europe offices down over 20%, against

high-single-digit drawdowns in US retail and industrial
and European residential. Fundamentals are supportive:
shrinking new supply and resilient occupier demand
should underpin a progressive, income-led recovery, but
the path is gradual, with no sector expected to regain its
peak before 2028 and offices to barely move (Figure 15).
Rates are the constraint. With 10y yields at multi-decade
highs on renewed inflation pressure and fiscal concerns,
private appraisal cap rates still sit near 2021 levels.

Marks have yet to absorb the move, so further outward
yield shift and headwinds on refinancing and liquidity lie
ahead.

### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Stronger Growth, Costlier Money
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
The latest GDP report and annual revisions show a sturdier U.S. economy than previously
reported. According to the Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA), real GDP grew at a 2.2%
annualized pace in Q2 compared to 1.5% previously estimated, and Q1 was revised
from 2.1% to 2.5%. Both 2024 and 2025 were also revised slightly higher. Most of the

first half of the year revision was in categories related to the Artificial Intelligence (AI)
buildout, but real consumer spending has also been revised slightly higher, accelerating
from 0.7% annualized growth in Q1 to 3.8% in Q2 despite surging energy prices and
slow hiring.
Spending in July and August remained much stronger than labor market conditions alone
would suggest, also averaging 3.7% annualized growth. Upside revisions to net interest

income and personal saving suggest households have benefited from more income than
was previously apparent, with personal saving revised higher back to 2023. The new data,
according to the BEA, show the January to July 2026 savings rate averaged 4.8% versus
3.3% prerevision, strengthening consumer spending underpinnings despite moderate
hiring, high energy prices and rising borrowing costs.
The BEA update also included inflation methodological changes related to categories

inflated by the AI boom—such as portfolio management fees and computer software and
accessories. These improved the inflation picture to some extent—August “core” PCE
inflation was reduced by 0.3 ppts to 3% YoY, bringing it closer to “core” consumer price
index inflation, which was 2.5% YoY and 2% on a three-month annualized basis. While still
above the 2% Federal Reserve (Fed) target, “core” PCE now appears stable at 3% year over
year since January rather than accelerating. These methodological changes are expected

to dampen pass-through of AI-related price pressures into “core” inflation and effects of
stock-market swings on inflation.
They also produced an encouraging improvement in recent PCE inflation momentum.
Three-month annualized “core” PCE inflation slowed from 4.3% at the peak of tariff
effects in February to 2.1% in August. For durable goods, this rate was down from 5.8%
in February to 1.5% in August, and, for services ex-energy and healthcare, it slowed from

4.2% to 2.2%, for example. Inflation is volatile on a three-month change basis, so it
remains to be seen whether this moderation continues without further Fed tightening.
For now, the trends in the San Francisco Fed’s cyclical versus acyclical inflation measures
diverged again in August. As expected, given softening wage growth, prices most sensitive
to the labor market have eased further. At 3% YoY, this inflation category is down from
8% in early 2023 and 4.6% two years ago is back to prepandemic levels and is likely

to ease more (Exhibit 1A). In contrast, supply bottlenecks, high energy prices and other
industry-specific factors have kept acyclical inflation on an uptrend. As shown in Exhibit
1B, the relentless increase in freight costs could prolong upside pressure on this category.
The diesel market has tightened in recent months, boosting its price by 70% YoY,
consistent with Brent oil at around $200 per barrel and large reverberations across
a wide swath of goods and services prices. A government-policy-related decline in

available truck drivers is also raising transportation costs more than expected. So are
tight ocean shipping capacity and longer/more expensive voyages due to the U.S./Iran
War. Supply-chain stress also increased as companies rushed to front-run potential new
tariff increases.
On the positive side for the inflation outlook, unit labor costs (ULC) are up modestly
as robust productivity growth has absorbed much of the wage growth, limiting a major
source of domestic inflation. Upside productivity growth revisions are likely to further

reduce ULC, which could help shift focus from energy and AI-related inflation to
eventual disinflationary effects once oil and refined fuel prices start to ease. Sustained
productivity gains would support growth and margins while limiting inflation and the
need for much higher interest rates.
Investment Implications
We favor selective Equity exposure
to companies with durable demand
and pricing power, while remaining
cautious about businesses most

exposed to fuel, freight, and
financing costs. We would also
gradually increase long-duration
exposure.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Surging nominal growth has boosted corporate revenues (up 13% YoY in Q2, according to the
BEA) while contained unit labor costs helped raise margins and profits more than expected.
With labor costs declining as a share of revenues, investment-related tax incentives and
rock-bottom net interest expense, pre-tax nonfinancial corporate profits as measured in GDP
accounts rose 21% YoY in Q2. Total corporate profits after-tax were up 18.2% YoY, according to

the BEA.
Surging AI-related imports and investment are keeping the business and profits cycle on
an uptrend both here and abroad. According to BofA Global Research, the global economic
cycle backdrop improved further in September, as reflected in a broad-based increase in its
Global Wave1 indicator. Broadening U.S. manufacturing strength, fueled by ongoing AI-driven
investment, a healthier orders backlog and low inventories suggest that the expansion has

room to run—a constructive environment for earnings. Typically, a Global Wave upswing is
consistent with rising earnings revisions ratios and equity price uptrends. According to the
Global Wave, global earnings growth expectations remain exceptionally strong.
Still, absent a resolution of war-related energy and transportation disruptions, the same
industrial strength intensifies inflation pressures. A prolonged supply shock raises the risk of
restrictive interest rates and weakening economic growth. In this context, the surge in the

10-year Treasury yield above 5% is starting to reflect more than optimism about U.S. growth
and strengthening productivity outlook. Oil’s tightest recent correlation with 10-year Treasury
yields in 35 years suggests a common energy-price thread behind rising yields in developed
countries.
Bond market spillovers from normalizing Japan yields and capital repatriation are another
common thread. Higher yields on foreign sovereign bonds such as in Japan—which has

escaped stagnation and deflation by intensely pursuing investment-led growth—weaken the
relative appeal of Treasurys. That distinction matters for portfolios. A yield increase driven
mainly by durable productivity gains would come with a stronger outlook for future income
and earnings. A yield increase driven by cost shocks and greater uncertainty raises financing
costs without the same benefit.
In sum, the economy remains more resilient than expected, but the bond market is also

charging more for an uncertain inflation outlook given untethered government debt and
cooling global demand for Treasurys spurred by geopolitical tensions and Japan capital
repatriation. The case for much further tightening becomes stronger if wage growth
strengthens, productivity lags, and unit labor costs reaccelerate, causing cyclical inflation to
also pick up steam. The case becomes weaker if that measure stays contained while fuel and
freight pressures recede or rising Treasury yields reduce asset prices and economic growth.

### Page 4

4 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Yields, Yen and Iran: What Haunts the “House”
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
We recently highlighted the three-front challenge facing Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent:
engineering a stronger yen, containing the 10-year Treasury yield and securing a diplomatic
resolution to the conflict with Iran.

Bessent recently has delivered one of the year’s more memorable lines while discussing
Treasury policy and the yen: “I have asymmetric information. I am the house now.” 2 Yet several
challenges remain: the 10-year Treasury yield is near levels last seen in the early 2000s, the
yen remains structurally weaker, and a phased agreement to reopen the Strait of Hormuz
appears remote. The resulting strain on energy markets keeps inflation risks elevated.

Put differently, oil prices, Treasury yields and the dollar-yen exchange rate are all trending
against Bessent’s objectives. Below, we examine how each variable is haunting his “house.”
Haunting the “House” is the Yen. Since the U.S. and Japan mounted a record $98.7B
coordinated intervention in late July after the yen weakened to a 40-year low near 164 per
dollar, the currency has appreciated roughly 4% against the dollar. The move was further
supported on September 18 when the BoJ raised rates by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%,

their highest level since the mid-1990s (Exhibit 2A). However, the split 7–2 vote and Governor
Ueda’s limited forward guidance leave the path of further tightening uncertain. The key
question is whether additional BoJ hikes can produce sustained appreciation in the yen, or
merely reinforce the temporary relief provided by the summer intervention.
Bessent, meanwhile, has repeatedly signaled his preference for a stronger yen. Partly because
yen stability is also a U.S. financing concern. Persistent currency weakness could prompt

Japan to support its currency by selling foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys—which would
be an unwelcome prospect as Washington partially relies on foreign capital flows to fund its
large fiscal deficits. That potential “haunts” the Treasury Secretary.  At precisely the moment
Washington needs foreign capital to finance its enormous fiscal deficits, it would be inopportune
for one of America’s largest creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities.

Exhibit 2: Charting the “Yentervention” and U.S. Treasury Yields Surge Across the Curve.
A) The Yen Regains Some Strength. B) 5-Year Forward Yields Hold Above 5%.

Haunting the “House” are Yields. The surge in yields runs counter to a White House intent
on lowering borrowing costs. The benchmark 10-year yield has broken above the 5% threshold
and climbed to levels not seen since 2007. At around 5.30%, it stands more than a full
percentage point above where it began the year (Exhibit 2B). The level is striking but so is the
speed of the move: Stronger-than-expected economic data, including a survey showing U.S.

2 September 8, 2026.
Investment Implications
While some key variables are
complicating the macro backdrop
(i.e., higher-for-longer yields/oil)
the combination of strong earnings
growth, robust consumer demand
(real final sales to private domestic
purchasers grew at 4.6% for Q2) and
ongoing capital investment suggests
that the fundamental backdrop for
U.S. Equities remains constructive.

### Page 5

5 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
business activity expanded at the fastest pace in more than five years, helped trigger an 18
bps surge in the 10-year yield—among its largest daily moves in decades. The 5-year yield also
topped 5% for the first time since 2007, while the 30-year yield reached 5.63%, its highest
level since 2002.
Compounding the move is inflation, running above the Fed’s 2% target for 67 months,

large fiscal deficits and the prospect of continued growth in Treasury issuance. Against this
backdrop, Treasury bought back $4.1 billion of long-dated debt, versus a maximum of $6
billion. That effort has done little to arrest the selloff. A subsequent auction priced nearly $4
billion of 7-year notes at 5.085%, the highest yield for that maturity in 33 years.
All in, the MOVE index, a key gauge of Treasury market volatility, jumped from 78 to 95 on

Wednesday September 23 and then to 104 the following day, its highest level since the early
days of the Iran war (Exhibit 3A).
Haunting the “House” is the Iran conflict. Early expectations that military pressure and
economic sanctions would quickly bring Iran to the negotiating table have yet to materialize.
Iranian officials have signaled that a full reopening of the Strait of Hormuz remains unlikely
while sanctions stay in place. Although talks between Washington and Tehran are continuing,

conflicting signals and lingering skepticism from both sides have kept tensions elevated.
As a result, oil prices have remained elevated in a range even as some Middle East supply
disruptions began to ease.
Startling is that Brent crude is up more than 60% this year, but refined product prices
have risen even faster. Ongoing disruptions to energy flows through the Strait of Hormuz,
combined with Ukrainian strikes on Russian refining infrastructure have tightened global

supplies of crude oil and refined fuels. Saudi Arabia has restored some drone-disrupted flows,
providing roughly 3.5 million barrels a day of relief (roughly 3% of global consumption).
The squeeze is mostly acute in diesel markets, where inventories remain constrained and
prices continue to climb (Exhibit 3B). U.S. diesel prices recently surpassed $6.50 per gallon,
the highest level on record, just as Russia has extended restrictions on diesel exports into

October. Against this backdrop, proposals to ban U.S. fuel exports risks worsening the
imbalance. According to S&P Global, an export ban could reduce refinery runs by nearly 2
million barrels per day and significantly cut gasoline production, potentially driving gasoline
and jet fuel prices even higher. As long as tensions persist, the oil impulse remains a key
impediment to structurally lower inflation.
Investment implications. Treasury Secretary Bessent increasingly sits at the intersection of

monetary policy, fiscal policy, foreign policy and national security. We remain constructive on
the U.S. economy and overweight U.S. Equities but are watching as these fronts challenge the
narrative. There is a great deal riding on the “house” versus the key macro variables of today.
Exhibit 3: Pulse Check on: Equity/Bond Volatility and Diesel/Gasoline Prices.

### Page 6

6 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
When It Comes to Attracting Foreign Capital, the U.S. Remains Exceptional
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
There may be no better vote of confidence in a country than the willingness of
foreigners to put their money there. And by that measurement, the U.S. remains the
exception—both portfolio and FDI inflows are currently running at record highs based

on the latest data. This dynamic is one key reason why we remain constructive on U.S.
assets over the longer term.
Notwithstanding all the handwringing about America’s fiscal deficits, tariffs and rising
government debt, global investors sunk some $942B in net purchases of U.S. Equities in
the 12 months to July, the highest rolling 12-month total on record. In Q2, net purchase
of U.S. Equities soared to $426B, up 62% from the same period a year ago (Exhibit 4A).

By country, flows were down from China—not surprisingly—but strong from Taiwan and
South Korea, who have taken to recycling their massive AI-generated trade surplus back
into the U.S. In the end, strong portfolio inflows suggest that international investors
remain willing to increase their exposure to the world's largest capital market—that’s
bullish for U.S. assets.
Meanwhile, Exhibit 4B highlights the growing push among foreign multinationals to be

inside the U.S., with FDI inflows totaling nearly $240B in the first six months of this year.
That is a staggering 103% jump from a year ago and means FDI inflows are on track for
a record year, estimated at $480B for 2026.
There are multiple factors behind surging inflows: One, due to rising U.S. trade
protectionism, foreign multinationals increasingly want to be “inside” the U.S. economy,
not outside. Two, within the $32 trillion-plus economy with extraordinarily consumer

purchasing power, foreign firms prefer to be closer to their customers via investment
than just trade (exports). Three, America’s massive investment cycle in AI, data centers,
semiconductors, power generation, etc., has created numerous potential investment
opportunities for many foreign firms that don’t want to miss out on America’s surging
capex cycle. Four, and finally, abundant energy supplies, deep capital markets and a highly
developed technological ecosystem have also induced more FDI inflows.

In the end, double-barreled capital inflows speak to the absolute and relative
attractiveness of the U.S. economy and capital markets. Stay invested in America—the
rest of the world is.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

ICE BofA MOVE Index is a market-implied measure of U.S. bond market volatility.
Consumer Price Index measures the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a market basket of consumer goods and services.
Baltic Dry Index is a daily shipping and trade index published by the Baltic Exchange in London that measures the cost of moving raw materials like iron ore, coal, and grain by sea.
Volatility Index (VIX) is a real-time market indicator that measures the stock market's expected volatility over the next 30 days.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.

## Document 275: Cliff Notes_ mulling the minutes (2026-10-21)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ mulling the minutes (2026-10-21).pdf

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: mulling the minutes
By Sian Fenner, Ryan Wells & Illiana Jain
11:10 October 09 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
The only data release of note in Australia this week was the October Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment
Survey, which reported a 4.7% drop in the headline index. At 80.4, sentiment has tumbled back to
deeply pessimistic levels, similar to the post-pandemic cost-of-living shock when readings were among

the weakest in the survey’s more than fifty-year history. Indeed, inflation and interest rate pressures are
once again clearly front of mind for consumers, with both the view on ‘family finances vs a year ago’

### Page 2

and ‘family finance next 12 months’ falling 8.0% and 6.4% in the month, respectively. Moreover, the
RBA’s September rate hike looks to have had a significant impact – sentiment tanked 20% between
those surveyed before the RBA decision and those surveyed afterwards.

Views on the economic outlook were relatively more resilient, but they still weakened, with the one-year
and five-year ahead sub-indexes falling 1.6% and 0.4% respectively. Consumers are becoming more
‘on edge’ about job security, albeit not outright alarmed. The unemployment rate remains relatively low
versus history, although it has drifted up to a cycle high of 4.6% as labour supply continues to outstrip
demand. We expect it to hold there in next week’s September Labour Force Survey – see our preview
for more detail. All told, with higher interest rates, renewed inflation pressures and a softening jobs
market all weighing at once, households are entering the final months of the year on the back foot.

Offshore, the focus was on US data.

Before turning to this week's releases, it is worth noting that September non-farm payrolls came in
softer than expected across most metrics. Employment rose by only 29k (89k expected), and there
were sizeable downward revisions to prior months, with a combined 60k removed from July and August
payrolls, leaving gains of just 31k and 29k respectively. The details pointed to weaker hiring in leisure &
hospitality and education & health services industries, although construction and manufacturing
employment continued to benefit from ongoing data-centre-related investment. The unemployment rate
edged up to 4.2%, with softer employment occurring alongside a rise in labour force participation to
61.8%. Meanwhile wage growth moderated to 0.1%mth (3.0%yr). Taken together, the report suggests
labour demand is cooling, with the three-month average pace of job creation slowing to around 50k.
However, softer payroll gains are not necessarily indicative of labour market deterioration. Lower
immigration and ageing demographics have reduced the pace of employment growth needed to keep
the unemployment rate stable. As such, the report is more consistent with a labour market that is
gradually normalising than one entering a meaningful downturn.

The September FOMC minutes reinforced a distinctly hawkish message, with participants noting they
had "not seen sufficient progress on lowering inflation in recent months" and expressing concern that
inflation risks remained "skewed to the upside". Policymakers highlighted that both core services and
core goods inflation remained elevated, with some attributing persistent goods price strength to the
effects of the AI buildout despite the sector's traditional disinflationary role. There was also concern
that, after more than five years of inflation above target, elevated inflation could begin to influence
inflation expectations and wage- and price-setting behaviour. In contrast, labour market conditions
were generally viewed as stable and "close to maximum employment". While a majority noted the
labour market had strengthened somewhat recently, several participants highlighted unusually low
dynamism, characterised by low hiring, low layoffs, a low job-finding rate and elevated long-term
unemployment. Importantly, participants generally judged that aggregate wage growth was moderate
and "consistent with inflation moving toward 2 percent", with several explicitly noting that "the labor
market was not currently a source of inflationary pressures".

### Page 3

Related articles
A key focus was the interaction between AI investment, productive capacity and inflation. While
participants broadly expected AI-related investment to boost productivity growth and potential output
over time, several warned that "the AI buildout could cause aggregate demand to outpace aggregate
supply over the medium term, putting upward pressure on inflation". Consistent with this view, some
participants had raised their estimate of the neutral policy rate and several judged the current policy
stance to be "not restrictive or only mildly restrictive". Against this backdrop, all participants supported
the September rate increase, and most assessed that another increase in the federal funds rate would
likely be appropriate by year-end, although future decisions would remain data dependent.

The September ISM Services PMI released this week painted a similar picture of an economy that
remains resilient but is gradually losing momentum. The headline index eased to 54.9 from 55.4 in
August, with the decline driven by a sharp fall in business activity and a 9.4pt drop in new export
orders, which slipped into contractionary territory for the first time in eight months. Labour market
conditions improved modestly with the employment index moving to broadly neutral territory 50.1 after
two months below 50, supported by elevated backlogs and still-solid demand. Respondent
commentary illustrated the mixed nature of labour demand, with some firms reporting they were "filling
positions vacated due to promotions or retirements", while others cited "restructuring due to efficiencies
gained using AI tools". Cost pressures also remained elevated, with the prices paid index rising to 74.0,
its highest level since July 2022 and around 18pts above its historical average, while fuel costs, tariffs
and supply chain constraints remained the most frequently cited concerns. Overall, the report suggests
services activity remains consistent with ongoing expansion, albeit at a slower pace, while labour
demand and pricing pressures remain firm.

Overall, the data supports our view that the FOMC will move to a broadly neutral policy stance by the
end of the year with one more rate hike, although the case for a back-to-back move in October has
become less clear cut.

The bank
for a changing
world
7

## Document 380: SpaceX与特斯拉合并，马斯克在下一盘多大的棋？

Source PDF: all report pdf/SpaceX与特斯拉合并，马斯克在下一盘多大的棋？.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-21
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

两家公司合计估值约 3.5 万亿美元，⽽⻢斯克或以不到四成经济权益掌握过半投票权。此次
合并远不⽌⽕箭和汽⻋，更涉及 AI 算⼒、芯⽚、能源、机器⼈与太空基础设施的全⾯整
合。然⽽， SpaceX 的 400 亿美元融资计划、监管审查与股东博弈，仍为这场潜在合并增添
变数。
摘要：
SpaceX 交出⼀份很难再⽤ " ⽕箭公司 " 概括的成绩单：第三季度， SpaceX 营收 146 亿美元，同⽐
增⻓ 161% ，净利润 44 亿美元，超出分析师平均预期逾⼀倍。
财报电话会上，⻢斯克确认： SpaceX 和特斯拉已分别成⽴由独⽴董事组成的特别委员会，讨论
可能的合并。两家公司合并估值约 3.5 万亿美元。
⻢ 斯 克 先 说 ⾃ ⼰ 是 在 " 雷 区 ⾥ 跳 踢 踏 舞 " 。 ⼏ 秒 之 后 还 是 给 了 答 案 ， 合 并 " 有 很 多 好 处 " 。
Deepwater Asset Management 的  Gene Munster 事后数了数，这句话他讲了⼤概三遍。
这让⼀个谈论已久的设想进⼊了正式评估阶段：彭博指出，特斯拉市值约  1.5 万亿美元，

SpaceX 约  2 万亿美元，合在⼀起  3.5 万亿。
会不会合并，已经不该是问题。巴克莱在  10 ⽉  14 ⽇的报告认为，两家合并的可能性 " 已经越来
越成为共识预期 " 。股东名册⾼度重叠， SpaceX 买特斯拉的  Cybertruck ，⼜通过  xAI 买公⽤事
业级储能电池。今年  9 ⽉的  All-In 峰会上，⻢斯克被问到为什么两家公司要分开，他反问： " 这
么多层⾯都在合作，谁能想象在如此紧密的合作下会采取什么⾏动。 "
消息传出后， SpaceX 盘后涨幅约 8% ，特斯拉仅涨约 1.8%—— 收购⽅涨得⽐被收购⽅更多。这
⼀差异反映出两类股东截然不同的盘算，也预⽰着这笔交易在正式落地之前，还有漫⻓的博弈
要⾛。
SpaceX 的 AI 业务已⼤到⽆法当作副业
SpaceX 第三季度的收⼊结构说明了合并讨论为何在此时升温。⼈⼯智能及算⼒业务收⼊ 75.6 亿
美元，较上年同期增⻓逾九倍，已⾸次超过 Starlink 所在的连接业务，季度营业利润从去年同期

亏损 17 亿美元转为盈利 4.31 亿美元。连接业务营收 52.5 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 69% ，⽬前仍贡献更
多利润，但增速明显慢于 AI 业务。相⽐之下，设计、制造和运营⽕箭与⻜船的传统发射业务营收
17.7 亿美元，仅增⻓ 6% 。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

增⻓的另⼀⾯是开⽀。第三季度， SpaceX 资本⽀出接近 196 亿美元，其中 AI 及算⼒业务约 164
亿美元。前九个⽉，公司经营现⾦流约 131 亿美元，购置物业、⼚房及设备⽀出约 481 亿美元。
单看 44 亿美元的季度净利润，很容易低估算⼒设施建设所需的真实资⾦规模。据报道， SpaceX
正在洽谈借款 400 亿美元⽤于采购英伟达芯⽚， CFO Bret Johnsen 表⽰公司会继续举债， " 有好
⼏个可⽤的债务资⾦池正在谈 " ，并给出明年⼀季度总营收最⾼ 370 亿美元的指引。
SpaceX ⽬前已是 Anthropic 、 Alphabet 等公司的主要算⼒与基础设施供应商， 8 ⽉还收购了代码
编辑⼯具 Cursor 。⼀家收⼊结构⾥ AI 占⽐最⾼、正在⼤举举债买卡的公司，⽤ " ⽕箭公司 " 来定义
已经远远不够。
协同逻辑清晰，但股权合并未必是唯⼀路径
⻢斯克列举的合作清单涵盖超级智能软件、芯⽚设计、 Terafab 、机器⼈、轨道数据中⼼和连接
服务。这些项⽬不断碰到同⼀批资源：芯⽚、能源、制造能⼒，以及能够持续⽀付建设成本的

资本。" 简化决策 " 是合并动机中排名靠前的⼀项 —— 许多跨公司项⽬需要两个董事会和两批股东
分别点头。
Terafab 是其中最具体的节点。按巴克莱测算，特斯拉计划先在奥斯汀建⼀座约 30 亿美元的研究
设施，随后跟进约 168 亿美元的⽣产⼚，两家公司很可能共同出资。这⾥产出的芯⽚将供给特斯
拉⾃动驾驶汽⻋和擎天柱机器⼈，同时也供给 SpaceX 的 AI 与轨道数据中⼼。⻢斯克此前已明确
表⽰， Terafab 的决策需要两家董事会各⾃审批， " 可能要花⼀阵⼦ " ，⽬标是 2028 年底量产。
10 ⽉ 15 ⽇亮相的新款 Roadster 搭载了 SpaceX 开发的冷⽓推进器，可完成倒退上坡、空中转向等
动作。Gene Munster 对那场发布会的评价是 " ⼀次⼯程炫技 " ，并说⻢斯克 " 等不及 " 要合并。
然⽽，业务能够深度合作，并不能直接证明股权必须合并。特别委员会需要回答的核⼼问题
是：合作产⽣的新增价值是否⾜以抵消交易成本、监管摩擦和整合⻛险。
" 特斯拉为 SpaceX 输⾎ " 的叙事，数字并不⽀撑

合并传闻中，⼀个直观的说法是：SpaceX ⼤建 AI 基础设施，特斯拉可以提供现⾦流。这⼀说法
抓住了 SpaceX 资本开⽀庞⼤的事实，却⾼估了特斯拉眼下可供调配的资⾦。
据彭博⼀致预期，特斯拉 2030 年⾃由现⾦流约 120 亿美元，⽽ SpaceX 预计届时消耗约 170 亿美
元 —— 两者⽅向相反。更近的数字同样不乐观：特斯拉第⼆季度⾃由现⾦流为负 11 亿美元；分
析师预测其第三季度⾃由现⾦流均值为负 45 亿美元， 2026 年全年约为负 97 亿美元。与此同时，
SpaceX 截⾄ 9 ⽉底持有约 513 亿美元现⾦及 386 亿美元有价证券，资⾦状况并不匮乏。
合并不会凭空增加⼀美元现⾦，它可能改变的是融资⽅式和资本配置权限 —— 哪些项⽬由合并
后的公司统⼀建设，哪些成本由不同业务共同承担，以及投资者愿意为这⼀整套计划提供多少
资本。
对⻢斯克⽽⾔，统⼀配置资源或许更⾼效；对特斯拉股东⽽⾔，问题在于汽⻋、储能和机器⼈
业务创造的价值，将如何分配给 SpaceX 的算⼒、卫星和航天项⽬。若交易⽅案说不清这⼀

点， " 协同 " 反⽽会成为资本开⽀失控的担忧。
控制权结构，可能是交易设计⾥最关键的⼀笔
分析师普遍认为，这笔交易在经济利益之外还有另⼀层逻辑 ——控制权。

### Page 3

巴克莱测算，⻢斯克持有 SpaceX 约 48% 的经济权益，通过每股 10 倍投票权的 B 类股掌握约 82%
的投票权；在特斯拉持股约 20% ，折算后接近 17% 的投票权。若 SpaceX 按当前估值以换股⽅式
收购特斯拉，他在合并后公司中⼤约持有 37% 的经济权益，控制约 56% 的投票权。 RBC 的⼝径
稍有差异，认为⻢斯克掌握 SpaceX 85% 的投票权，但⽅向⼀致：交易会把 " 经济权益不到四
成、投票权过半 " 这个结构复制到⼀家 3.5 万亿美元量级的公司⾝上。
⻢斯克曾公开表⽰，他希望持有特斯拉约 25% 的股权，才⾜以维持对 AI 和机器⼈战略的影响⼒，
⽽⽬前仍有差距。这笔交易可以利⽤ SpaceX 的双层股权结构补上这个缺⼝。
这⼀点直接影响谈判难度。⻢斯克本⼈已承认存在利益冲突，不能参与特别委员会的讨论。委
员会不仅要评估业务协同，还要确定各⾃估值、换股⽐例、可能的溢价，以及不同类别股东在
合并后各⾃拥有多少经济权益和投票权。
真正握着否决权的，是华盛顿

路透社将地缘政治和监管列为合并的 " 最⼤挑战 " ：SpaceX 是美国军⽅、情报机构和 NASA 的主
要承包商， Starshield 已被⽤于战场通信，⻢斯克和特朗普均否认对其设限，但这套系统的军事
敏感性已⽏庸置疑。
因此，对合并的审查维度将从反垄断扩展到国家安全。反垄断审查、双⽅股东与董事会批准、
外国投资审查，三道程序中任何⼀道卡住，交易的形态都会发⽣变化。这种类型的交易在商业
史上没有先例，也不存在现成的监管模板。
盘⾯给出答案，但定价有待细化
特别委员会消息确认后， SpaceX 盘后最多涨约 8.6% ，特斯拉仅涨约 1.8% ， SPCX 盘前⼀度报
161.18 美元，较收盘价⾼约 9% 。
Space Capital 的 Chad Anderson 给出了⼀种叙事：合并后的公司将是唯⼀拥有完整 " 物理 AI
栈 " 的企业，从芯⽚制造到轨道数据中⼼，再到地⾯⾃主设备，没有竞争对⼿有对应的完整体
系。他的机构是 SpaceX 的早期投资⼈。
RBC 给予特斯拉 480 美元的⽬标价，较 368 美元附近的收盘价隐含不到三成的上⾏空间。对于特

斯拉股东⽽⾔，算盘并不难打：⽤确定性的汽⻋周期换成⼀家 AI 算⼒公司的⻓期期权。但这笔换
算是否划算，取决于换股⽐例和合并后的资本分配⽅案，⽽这两份⽂件⽬前都还不存在。
值得注意的是，作为收购货币的 SPCX 于今年 6 ⽉ IPO ，定价 135 美元，⾸⽇开盘 150 美元，四个
⽉后股价基本回到起点。⽤⼀只上市不久的股票作为收购对价，其定价基准仍主要依赖投⾏模
型，⼆级市场尚未经过反复交易的充分检验。若只有愿景继续升温⽽交易条款迟迟缺席，股价
反应很难替代对换股⽐例和资本计划的实质审查。
眼下最该盯的四件事
⾸先，是特别委员会的构成与独⽴性。 两家公司都表⽰由独⽴董事组成委员会，但成员是谁、
有⽆聘请独⽴财务顾问、顾问是否出具公允意⻅，将决定这份⽅案在股东眼⾥是 " ⻢斯克的意
思 " 还是 " 董事会的判断 " 。
其次， 12 ⽉ 3 ⽇股东年会如何使⽤。 特斯拉已将 2026 年股东年会定于 12 ⽉ 3 ⽇。按⽬前披露的信
息，不能将这场年会视为已安排好的合并投票。赶在年会前宣布交易意向，反对票更容易组

织；错开宣布，则⾯临更⻓的监管窗⼝和更多变数。巴克莱的分析称，若选在 10 ⽉底或 11 ⽉初

### Page 4

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
343 收藏 分享到 :
写评论
图⽚
宣布，股东在年度会上投赞成票反⽽更难 —— 这与 " 抢在窗⼝关闭前⽣⽶煮成熟饭 " 的直觉正好相
反。
接着，那 400 亿美元借款的条款与时序。 若这笔资⾦在合并敲定之前先⾏落地，意味着 SpaceX
打算在交易完成前就锁定算⼒资本开⽀，合并对 SpaceX 的意义就更偏向补充现⾦流，⽽⾮共同
投资。
最后， Terafab 和特斯拉财报的实际数字。 得州设施的⼟地、环评、设备订购，任何⼀环慢下
来， 2028 年底量产⽬标就要后移。⽽特斯拉第三季度财报将同期呈现资本⽀出、⾃由现⾦流，
以及管理层对 Terafab 、机器⼈和算⼒投资的最新表态 —— 若现⾦消耗继续扩⼤，股东对合并后
资本纪律的要求只会更⾼。
⻢斯克想推进的，可能是⼀家从芯⽚和能源供给，到算⼒、连接⽹络，再到机器⼈应⽤都能⾃

⾏协调的公司。 这盘棋有多⼤，已经能从 SpaceX 的收⼊结构和投资规模中看⻅。它能否让股东
受益，则要等特别委员会拿出价格、治理安排和可核算的协同收益。眼下最该盯的，是特斯拉
财报⾥的现⾦流，以及两家公司下⼀份正式交易⽂件。

• The global landscape remains complex, shaped by AI, 
energy prices and tariffs. 
• Taiwan’s AI cycle remains strong, with robust AI 
exports and investment and an intact semiconductor 
competitive moat. 
• AI is generating broader domestic spillovers, with 
wealth effects and wage growth supporting 
consumption. 
• Taiwan is cushioning the energy shock by diversifying 
energy imports and absorbing much of the increase in 
upstream costs. 
• AI-driven growth is likely to moderate from 4Q26 into

higher global rates lift investment costs. 
• Energy remains a n inflation risk, with domestic

Taiwan: AI, energy and 4Q outlook October 13, 2026

Global backdrop

The global landscape remains complex, 
shaped by the interplay of AI, energy 
prices and tariffs, with significant 
implications for growth, inflation and 
financial conditions.

1) AI

The debate over the durability of the AI 
investment boom continues.  Tailwinds 
include technological advances, broader 
adoption, and s trategic and geopolitical 
considerations. Headwinds range from 
uncertainty over investment returns  and 
hyperscaler financing pressure to 
physical bottlenecks , safety concerns, 
and regulatory uncertainty.

Hyperscaler financing pressure has 
attracted increasing attention . In the 
early stages of the AI investment boom, 
hyperscaler capex was largely financed 
by internal cash flow. This is now 
changing, as free cash flow is expected 
to turn negative from 4Q, requiring major 
players to rely on debt issuance and 
other external financing increasingly.

At the same time, higher global interest 
rates are raising the cost of AI 
investment. The Fed resumed rate hikes 
at its September meeting, while global 
bond yields remain under upward 
pressure amid persistent inflation and 
elevated public and private borro wing 
needs. Our baseline assumes US rates 
will rise by 50bps this year and a further 
25bps in 2027 to 4.50%, with global 
long-term rates also remaining under 
pressure. This could increasingly weigh 
on the economics and pace of AI 
investment.

US-China AI competition is also drawing 
increasing attention.  Across the five 
layers of the AI ecosystem, China is 
making rapid progress in energy, 
infrastructure, AI models and 
applications, supported by its large scale, 
cost-effective domestic manufacturing 
supply chains and huge consumer 
market, which provides a wide range of 
real-world scenarios for AI adoption. The 
emergence of a more integrated and 
cost-competitive Chinese AI ecosystem 
could increasingly challe nge the US 
ecosystem, which currently operates at a 
relatively higher cost.

China, however, remains relatively weak 
in semiconductors.  Around half of 
China's domestic AI chip market still 
relies on foreign suppliers, including 
lower-end NVIDIA chips. This 
semiconductor bottleneck remains a key 
constraint on China's AI development. In 
our view, Taiwanese semiconductor 
companies' competiti ve moat therefore 
remains largely intact in the near term.

to 39% in 2027. Similarly, global 
semiconductor sales remained strong 
through 3Q, while WSTS expects growth 
to moderate from 90% this year to 27 % 
in 2027.

2) Energy

The US -Iran conflict remains at a 
standoff, keeping the energy -price 
outlook highly uncertain.  New 
developments are likely after the US 
midterm elections in November, with 
both further escalation and de -
escalation possible. Crude oil prices have 
remained elevated at around 
USD100/bbl amid a persistent 
geopolitical risk premium . In contrast, 
refined product prices have risen more 
sharply as refining disruptions and 
tighter product logistics have amplified 
the supply shock. Natural gas prices 
have also increased sharply, reflecting 
disruptions to LNG supply and limited 
alternative shipping routes.

Taiwan has been actively diversifying 
its energy import sources to cushion the 
impact. LNG imports from Qatar have 
fallen to zero since April, while imports 
from the US and other suppliers have 
surged to offset the shortfall, helping 
maintain stable domestic electricity 
supply.

3) Tariffs

US tariff actions are likely to accelerate 
after the US midterm elections in 
November. Two major tariff measures 
remain in the pipeline. The Section 301 
forced-labor tariff was implemented in 
July, while final tariff action under the 
Section 301 overcapacity investigation, 
which covers 16 economies, remains 
pending. Meanwhile, Phase 1 of the 
Section 232 semiconductor tariffs was 
implemented in January, while Phase 2, 
covering a broader range of 
semiconductor-related products, 
remains pending.

Taiwan has secured a relatively 
favourable framework through its trade 
and investment agreements with the 
US. Taiwan signed an Agreement on 
Reciprocal Trade (ART) and an 
Investment MOU with the US earlier this 
year. The ART establishes a 15% US tariff 
rate on Taiwanese goods, while the MOU 
provides Taiwan with preferential 
treatment under Section 232, including 
for semiconductors. The ART has yet to 
enter into force, and it remains to be 
seen how the final Section 301 tariff 
burden will interact with the 15% ART 
framework. Meanwhile, major Taiwanese 
companies such as TSMC are expanding 
their US investments under the 
Investment MOU, which could support 
relatively favourable treatment for 
Taiwan when broader Section 232 
semiconductor tariffs are introduced.

Taiwan 4Q outlook

Taiwan has navigated the complex 
global environment relatively well so far 
this year, supported by robust AI exports 
and investment, diversified energy 
imports, and efforts to absorb higher 
upstream energy costs. Some 
normalisation is likely in 4Q26 –2027, as 
the AI -driven growth boost moderates 
and domestic energy prices face greater 
pressure to adjust.

1) Growth

GDP growth remains broadly on track 
with our expectations. High -frequency 
indicators suggest that GDP growth 
should remain around 12% yoy in 3Q, 
broadly in line with 12.9% in 2Q but below 
the 15.4% peak in 1Q. Growth is expected 
to moderate to a high -single-digit pace 
from 4Q onwards. We maintain our full -
year GDP growth forecasts at 11.6% for 
2026 and 5.6% for 2027.

The AI cycle remains the key driver of 
Taiwan's growth.  Information and 
communication products and electronic 
components accounted for nearly 80% 
of total exports in the first nine months 
of this year, while manufacturing 
accounts for nearly 50% of gross fixed

capital formation. From 4Q onwards, 
export growth is expected to moderate 
from exceptionally strong levels to a 
more normal but still robust pace, 
broadly following the trajectory of 
global hyperscaler capex and 
semiconductor sales. Investment is likely 
to follow a similar path.

For now, Taiwanese technology 
companies continue to report robust 
revenue growth, led by semiconductors, 
ODMs/EMS, and PCs and servers. 
Capacity utilisation at leading Taiwanese 
foundries has also continued to rise, 
suggesting that investment demand 
remains strong.

The AI boom is also beginning to 
generate broader spillovers to the 
domestic economy. The AI-driven equity 
rally has generated significant wealth 
effects, with the TAIEX up 70% year-to-
date and Taiwan becoming the world's 
fifth-largest stock market. Taiwanese 
households have benefited from the 
appreciation of financial assets, with 
equities and mutual funds accounting for 
nearly 30% of total household financial 
assets. The equity rally has also 
generated positive income effects. Wage 
growth has picked up, led by the services 
sector, particularly financial services, as 
strong earnings at financial 
institutions—especially securities 
firms—have prompted higher 
compensation to retain talent. These 
wealth and income gains should support 
household consumption, creating a 
positive feedback loop that broadens the 
benefits of the AI boom across the wider 
economy.

                                                                                                                                            7

2) Inflation

CPI inflation is also tracking broadly in 
line with our forecast. Headline CPI 
remained elevated at 2.4% yoy in 3Q, up 
from 1.2% in 1Q and 2.2% in 2Q. We 
maintain our forecast for headline CPI 
inflation to remain close to 2.5% in 4Q, 
and retain our full-year forecasts at 2.0% 
for 2026 and 2.1% for 2027.

Energy inflation remains the main 
upside risk.  The government has 
absorbed much of the increase in energy 
costs through state -owned CPC and 
Taipower, limiting the pass -through to 
domestic consumers. With losses at both 
companies widening, the government 
will need to decide whether to provide 
additional subsidies to cover the losses 
or allow further increases in domestic 
energy prices. The September re view 
postponed the adjustment to the 
December meeting, when the overall 
situation will be reassessed after the 
November local elections. Taipower's  
latest review indicated that electricity 
rates would need to rise by 12.8%. A hike 
in electricity prices could lift inflation 
expectations and generate second -
round effects, particularly as the labour 
market tightens and wage pressures 
increase.

3) Monetary policy

The central bank kept the policy 
discount rate unchanged at 2.00% at its 
September meeting, in line with our 
expectations. We continue to expect a 
modest 12.5bp rate hike at the 
December meeting, bringing the policy 
rate to 2.125%.  By then, CPI inflation is 
likely to have remained above 2% for 
around six consecutive months. The 
possibility of an increase in electricity 
prices following the November local 
elections could further lift inflation 
expectations, strengthening the case for 
a December rate hike.

Financial conditions have already 
tightened despite the absence of a 
policy rate hike.  M2 growth remained 
around 7% yoy in 3Q, well below double-
digit nominal GDP growth. At the same 
time, bank loan growth has accelerated 
to more than 10% yoy, driven by strong

corporate credit demand, particularly AI-
related fundraising by technology 
companies. Tighter financial conditions 
are also evident in rising market interest 
rates, including commercial paper rates 
and government bond yields.

The central bank has continued to ease 
housing credit controls gradually. At its 
September meeting, it raised the LTV 
cap on second -home mortgage loans to 
70%, the second easing of housing credit 
controls this year. There is scope for 
further easing, including removing the 
LTV cap on second-home loans or raising 
the LTV caps for third - and fourth-home 
loans and high -value properties, which 
currently stand at 30%. However, the 
central bank is likely to proceed 
cautiously, given concerns that a faster 
relaxation could reignite  speculative 
demand and put renewed upward 
pressure on housing prices.

4) Housing

A gradual and modest recovery in the 
housing market is expected from 4Q26 
into 2027.  The existing -home market 
has already shown early signs of 
recovery, with Sinyi  housing prices 
returning to slightly positive yoy growth 
since 2Q. Total building transactions also 
returned to positive yoy growth in 3Q. 
The easing of credit controls should help 
reinforce this modest recovery trend.

The new -home market remains under 
pressure. The number of new units under 
construction has started to decline since 
2023, while new completions are  only 
beginning to ease this year . Demand 
remains weak, as potential buyers 
continue to wait for lower prices. Unsold 
new homes stood at 113K units at end -
2025, compared with a historical normal 
level of around 80K, implying excess 
inventory of roughly 30K units. At the 
current pace of new -home transactions, 
it would take roughly one year to 
normalize the existing excess inventory, 
and longer when factoring in the new 
supply pipeline. Some industry experts 
estimate that it could take as long as 3.8 
years digest current inventories fully 
(here).

CENTRAL BANK MEETINGS

People Bank of China (October 20)

We expect the PBOC to keep the 1Y LPR 
unchanged next week . Industrial 
production is expected to edge up from 
5.2% yoy in August to 5.3% in September, 
supported by firm external demand. 
Exports stayed strong, rising 36.3% yoy 
in September, driven by the regional AI -
electronics upcycle.

Domestic momentum, however, remains 
uneven. Retail sales growth is expected 
to moderate to 0.3% yoy  in September. 
Household sentiment is weak amid 
uncertain job prospects, slower income 
growth and elevated precautionary 
savings, while subdued property prices 
continue to weigh on household wealth 
and consumption. Investment also stays 
weak, with fixed as set investment 
expected to contract further by 7.5% yoy 
ytd in September. Corporate investment 
is cautious amid the anti -involution 
campaign and geopolitical tensions, 
while the property sector continues to be 
a major drag.

Policymakers are likely to maintain a 
measured policy stance, relying on 
targeted fiscal support to sustain growth 
rather than further rate cuts.

Bank Indonesia and Bangko Sentral ng 
Pilipinas (Oct 21 and 22).

Bank Indonesia is expected to extend its 
pause, as they lean on non-rate measures, 
including intervention and hedging 
facilities to help the currency.  On Friday, 
MSCI signaled a  potential shift in 
Indonesia’s status to a standalone 
status, rather than a reclassification to 
frontier market , after consultations are 
complete in mid -2027. Any ensuing risk -
off move in the currency , would likely 
prompt greater central bank vigilance 
and intervention.

Our base case for the benchmark rate to 
be held unchanged this quarter , backed 
by a lower conviction around back -to-
back US rate hikes in Oct and relative 
rupiah stability. Separately, the 
government announced additional 
support for low-income households in 4Q 
by way of in -kind assistance and wage 
subsidies. This year’s fiscal deficit i s 
likely to close in at -2.85% of GDP, close 
to our forecast at -2.9%, but few 
uncertainties facing the 2027 math (note).

We expect a 25bp hike from the BSP to 
5.25% in October , after three rate 
increases earlier in the year. Rising by the 
highest pace in Asia, Philippines inflation 
surged to 7.2% y/y in September from 6.1% 
in August, matching this year's peak and 
approaching a three -year high.  Inflation 
risks remain tilted to the upside, driven by 
further public transport fare adjustments, 
elevated oil prices, recent wage hikes 
and peso depreciation. Fiscal space is, 
meanwhile, limited to support 
households, via subsidies and/ instate 
price caps. Governor Remolona also 
signalled that the BSP remains ready to 
act on rates to contain inflation. With 
inflationary pressures likely to be 
persistent, the likelihood of further hikes 
in 4Q26 rises.

### Page 3

The Week Ahead October 16, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         3

Bank of Korea (October 22)

The Bank of Korea is expected to hold its 
policy rate at 3.00%, following back -to-
back rate hikes in July and August. AI -
driven growth momentum remains strong, 
as reflected in September export growth 
of around 80% YoY. However, the 
urgency for further tightening has eased, 
with CPI inflation stabilising at 2.9% YoY 
in September. The recent KOSPI 
correction and continued KRW strength 
also reduce the need for another near -
term hike. We maintain our call for a final 
25bp rate hike in November, bringing the 
policy rate to 3.25% by year-end.

FORTHCOMING DATA 
RELEASES

Japan

September trade and CPI data are due 
shortly. Export growth is expected to 
remain solid at 24% YoY, supported by 
continued strength in AI -related 
semiconductor exports. CPI inflation is 
projected to rise notably to 2.6% YoY, 
driven by the phase -out of publ ic utility 
subsidies, higher global energy prices, 
and persistent inflationary pressures 
from a weak yen. The BOJ is likely to 
increasingly acknowledge that its 2% 
inflation target is within reach. However, 
it is expected to keep rates unchanged 
at 1.25% at its October 29–30 meeting to 
assess the impact of the latest rate hike 
in September.

Taiwan

September export orders and industrial 
production data are due shortly. Export 
orders are expected to sustain 60% YoY 
growth, while industrial production is 
projected to expand by 20% YoY. The 
global AI boom faces growing headwinds 
from hyperscalers’ cash flow pressures, 
central bank rate hikes, and higher bond 
yields. However, our high -frequency 
indicators across AI demand (token 
expenditure), investment (hyperscaler  
capex and data centre construction 
spending), and supply (semiconductor 
sales and exports) show no clear signs of 
a significant slowdown so far.

Malaysia

Malaysia’s external demand likely 
remained strong in September 2026, 
while inflation edged up slightly. We 
expect export growth to sustain its 
robust expansion at 45% yoy , with 
momentum anchored by strong demand 
for electrical and electronics goods. Oil 
& gas shipments likely remained buoyant. 
However, high base effects prevented a 
faster pace of expansion.

We expect headline inflation to rise 
modestly to 2.1% yoy in September 2026. 
The renewed increase in global oil prices 
likely contributed to higher increases in 
domestic energy costs, although fuel 
subsidies continued to limit the cost 
pass-through and keep underlying 
inflationary pressures contained. Food 
inflation likely also firmed, with adverse 
weather conditions associated with El 
Nino posing upside risks.

### Page 4

The Week Ahead October 16, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         4

Singapore

We expect Singapore’s core and 
headline inflation to edge up slightly to 
2.3% yoy and 2.4% yoy , respectively, in 
September 2026, following modest 
increases to 2.2% yoy and 2.3% yoy in 
August. Inflationary pressures likely 
reflected firm private transport price 
increases amid higher petrol prices 
despite slower car price growth , the 
ongoing pass -through of higher global 
energy prices to fuel -exposed services 
items, positive contribution from 
elevated electricity & gas prices, firmer 
food inflation, and a slight increase in 
still-muted accommodation inflation. 
Firm, albeit not acc elerating, inflation in 
September 2026, which may be close to 
its peak in the Monetary Authority of 
Singapore’s assessment, likely validated 
its decision to maintain SGD NEER policy 
settings at the October 2026 review, 
following two proactive tightening 
moves in April and July.

Hong Kong

CPI is projected to edge up from 1.7% yoy 
in August to 1.9% in September, 
supported by improving consumption 
sentiment and higher global energy 
prices. Mainland visitor arrivals 
increased by 29.0% yoy in September on 
a daily average basis, providing furthe r 
support to retail spending and 
consumer-related services. Meanwhile, 
renewed conflict in the Middle East 
during the month has pushed up global 
energy prices, adding to upward 
pressure on utility and transport costs.

## Document 405: Triển vọng TTCK Việt Nam 4Q2026 (2026-10-07)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Triển vọng TTCK Việt Nam 4Q2026 (2026-10-07).pdf
Report source: KBSV (Strategy)
Date: 2026-10-07
Pages: 27
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Trần Đức Anh 
Giám đốc Kinh tế Vĩ mô & Chiến lược thị trường 
anhtd@kbsec.com.vn 
07/10/2026

Triển vọng TTCK 4Q2026 
Tích lũy trong vùng nhiễu động

Chúng tôi dự báo vùng điểm hợp lý của chỉ số VNIndex thời điểm cuối năm 2026 
xuống 1,800 điểm, giảm mạnh từ mức 1,950 điểm đưa ra trong báo cáo gần nhất. 
Vùng điểm này tương ứng:

- Tăng trưởng EPS 2026 so với cùng kỳ của thị trường chung ở mức 33% 
(tăng mạnh so với mức 25% của báo cáo gần nhất), chủ yếu nhờ kỳ vọng 
vào các chính sách thúc đẩy kinh tế của Chính phủ, và lợi nhuận tăng 
trưởng đột biến ở VIC (xem thêm phần Triển vọng hoạt động kinh doanh).

- Mức P/E hợp lý của chỉ số VNIndex thời điểm cuối năm ở mức 13.2 (giảm 
nhẹ so với mức 13.5 thời điểm hiện tại - phản ánh quan điểm các yếu tố 
rủi ro sẽ tiếp tục duy trì). Đây là mức định giá thấp so với bình quân 5 năm 
gần nhất ở 15.1. Nếu loại bỏ yếu tố đột biến ở nhóm Vingroup, chỉ số P/E 
điều chỉnh hiện đã rơi xuống 10.9, thấp hơn các đáy gần nhất năm 2020, 
2022 và 2025. Trong bối cảnh kinh tế Việt Nam duy trì tốc độ tăng trưởng 
cao, lợi nhuận các doanh nghiệp niêm yết liên tục bứt phá, vùng định giá 
này mở ra cơ hội đầu tư trung/dài hạn. Chúng tôi cho rằng ngay khi các rủi 
ro vĩ mô quốc tế (xoay quanh vấn đề Iran), và trong nước (xoay quanh vấn 
đề lãi suất) hạ nhiệt, thị trường chung sẽ có cơ hội tái định giá lên mức cao 
hơn đáng kể so với thời điểm hiện tại. Dù vậy, khác với báo cáo gần nhất 
vào tháng 7, ở báo cáo này, chúng tôi đẩy lui thời điểm các yếu tố rủi ro vĩ 
mô hạ nhiệt sang năm 2027, đồng nghĩa với quan điểm thận trọng hơn về 
thị trường cho quý 4 2026.

Cụ thể, đối với vấn đề lãi suất, áp lực lạm phát lớn dần, đi kèm nhu cầu vốn cao 
phục vụ hoạt động đầu tư cơ sở hạ tầng sẽ khiến mặt bằng lãi suất duy trì ở mức 
cao cho phần còn lại của năm 2026 (dù thanh khoản hệ thống ghi nhận sự cải thiện 
đáng kể trong 1 quý gần đây). Đối với vấn đề xung đột Trung Đông, các cuộc đàm 
phán vẫn đang bế tắc và ít có khả năng dòng chảy dầu mỏ tại khu vực này được nối 
lại ngay trong năm nay.

Chủ đề đầu tư chúng tôi lựa chọn cho 4Q2026 là đầu tư công, chính sách tiền tệ hỗ 
trợ, và xung đột Iran.

I. Triển vọng TTCK 4Q2026 
TTCK Việt Nam đi ngang, giảm nhẹ 
trong 9 tháng đầu năm 
 TTCK Việt Nam biến động giằng co, giảm điểm nhẹ trong 9 tháng đầu năm với 
diễn biến có thể chia làm 2 giai đoạn chính. Giai đoạn 1 kéo dài đến gần cuối 
quý 1, thị trường đi ngang trong 2 tháng đầu, trước khi lao dốc mạnh từ cuối 
tháng 2 trước ảnh hưởng bởi xung đột tại Trung Đông bùng phát. Giai đoạn 2 là 
khoảng thời gian còn lại, thị trường xuất hiện các nhịp biến động mạnh, 
tăng/giảm đan xen, chịu ảnh hưởng bởi thông tin về giá dầu, biến động lãi suất 
trong nước, kỳ vọng về nâng hạng thị trường và các yếu tố cơ bản khác. Tính 
chung 9 tháng, VNIndex gần như đi ngang, giảm nhẹ 0.9% so với cuối 2025, 
trong khi thanh khoản bình quân toàn thị trường giảm xấp xỉ 12% so với cùng 
kỳ.

Với diễn biến căng thẳng tại Trung Đông vẫn đang dai dẳng, đồng thời dự báo 
mặt bằng lãi suất sẽ khó xuất hiện xu hướng hạ nhiệt rõ nét trong năm 2026, dù 
vẫn có những điểm sáng vĩ mô và KQKD khả quan của doanh nghiệp, các dự 
báo của chúng tôi nhìn chung có phần thận trọng hơn so với dự báo gần nhất. 
Cụ thể:

Lợi nhuận các doanh nghiệp niêm 
yết dự báo tăng trưởng mạnh

Chúng tôi điều chỉnh dự báo tăng trưởng EPS toàn thị trường cho năm 2026 lên 
mức 33% YoY, chủ yếu nhờ phản ánh mức tăng 200% kế hoạch lợi nhuận sau 
thuế của VIC (loại trừ mức tăng trưởng đột biến này của VIC, tăng trưởng EPS 
toàn thị trường ở mức 17% YoY). Đóng góp chính đến từ mức tăng của một số 
nhóm có tỷ trọng lớn: Bất động sản (+79% YoY), Nguyên vật liệu (+44% YoY) và 
Hàng tiêu dùng thiết yếu (+27% YoY). Mức tăng trưởng EPS cao, tương đồng với 
số liệu tăng trưởng kinh tế vĩ mô tích cực, là động lực quan trọng hỗ trợ thị 
trường trong trung/dài hạn.

Bối cảnh vĩ mô quốc tế còn nhiều 
bất định

Bối cảnh kinh tế toàn cầu trong giai đoạn cuối năm vẫn chịu áp lực lớn từ xung 
đột địa chính trị ở Trung Đông và động thái điều hành chính sách của Fed. Đàm 
phán Mỹ – Iran chưa đạt tiến triển đáng kể, trong khi giao tranh tiếp diễn và 
hoạt động vận chuyển dầu bị gián đoạn, làm gia tăng rủi ro thiếu hụt nguồn 
cung và khiến giá dầu được dự báo tiếp tục neo cao trong thời gian tới. Giá 
năng lượng duy trì ở mức cao, cùng với áp lực lạm phát chưa hạ nhiệt rõ rệt, 
khiến Fed tiếp tục duy trì lập trường thận trọng và không loại trừ khả năng tiếp 
tục thắt chặt chính sách tiền tệ trong quý 4.

Mặt bằng lãi suất huy động dự báo 
duy trì mức cao trong quý 4

Chúng tôi duy trì đánh giá tích cực về triển vọng tăng trưởng kinh tế Việt Nam 
năm 2026, với GDP dự kiến tăng 8.5–9.0%, dựa trên kỳ vọng cán cân thương 
mại cải thiện, dòng vốn FDI tiếp tục hỗ trợ tăng trưởng và đầu tư công được đẩy 
mạnh. Tuy nhiên, giá năng lượng là rủi ro đáng chú ý, có thể gây thêm áp lực 
lên cán cân thương mại và lạm phát, qua đó ảnh hưởng đến triển vọng tăng 
trưởng trong thời gian tới. 
Áp lực thiếu hụt thanh khoản ngắn hạn đã có dấu hiệu hạ nhiệt, trong khi áp 
lực huy động vốn trung và dài hạn vẫn hiện hữu khi nhu cầu vốn của nền kinh 
tế dự kiến ở mức cao. Do đó, nhìn về cuối năm, chúng tôi cho rằng lãi suất chưa 
thể xuất hiện xu hướng giảm rõ rệt, nhưng kỳ vọng có thể ổn định quanh mặt 
bằng hiện tại nhờ hoạt động giải ngân đầu tư công tiếp tục hỗ trợ dòng tiền và 
NHNN vẫn còn dư địa điều tiết thanh khoản trong bối cảnh áp lực tỷ giá không 
lớn và áp lực lạm phát vẫn trong tầm kiểm soát.
